Indeed, the streets were too crowded and too ill lighted to expose very readily a careful spy, if such there should be at his heels. He reached his lodging in safety, and leaned his purchase against the wall, rather relieved, strong as he was, to be rid of its weight; then, lighting his pipe, threw himself on the couch, and was soon lapt in the folds of one of his haunting dreams.

===Chinese===
科斯莫小心翼翼地把这件宝货捧回家去。但一路上他总有一种不自在的感觉，仿佛被人监视着、尾随着。他一再四下张望，却看不出任何足以证实疑心的迹象。的确，街上人烟稠密，灯火又暗，倘若真有一个用心的密探跟在脚后，也不易轻易暴露。他平安到了寓所，把买来的东西靠墙立好；他虽然膂力过人，卸下这重负，也觉松了一大口气；随后点起烟斗，往躺榻上一倒，不久便被一个缠绵不去的旧梦的褶层裹挟而去。

===Original===
He returned home earlier than usual the next day, and fixed the mirror to the wall, over the hearth, at one end of his long room. He then carefully wiped away the dust from its face, and, clear as the water of a sunny spring, the mirror shone out from beneath the envious covering. But his interest was chiefly occupied with the curious carving of the frame. This he cleaned as well as he could with a brush; and then he proceeded to a minute examination of its various parts, in the hope of discovering some index to the intention of the carver. In this, however, he was unsuccessful; and, at length, pausing with some weariness and disappointment, he gazed vacantly for a few moments into the depth of the reflected room. But ere long he said, half aloud: "What a strange thing a mirror is! and what a wondrous affinity exists between it and a man's imagination! For this room of mine, as I behold it in the glass, is the same, and yet not the same. It is not the mere representation of the room I live in, but it looks just as if I were reading about it in a story I like. All its commonness has disappeared. The mirror has lifted it out of the region of fact into the realm of art; and the very representing of it to me has clothed with interest that which was otherwise hard and bare; just as one sees with delight upon the stage the representation of a character from which one would escape in life as from something unendurably wearisome. But is it not rather that art rescues nature from the weary and sated regards of our senses, and the degrading injustice of our anxious everyday life, and, appealing to the imagination, which dwells apart, reveals nature in some degree as she really is, and as she represents herself to the eye of the child, whose everyday life, fearless and unambitious, meets the true import of the wonder-teeming world around him, and rejoices therein without questioning? That skeleton, now—I almost fear it, standing there so still, with eyes only for the unseen, like a watchtower looking across all the waste of this busy world into the quiet regions of rest beyond. And yet I know every bone and every joint in it as well as my own fist. And that old battle-axe looks as if any moment it might be caught up by a mailed hand, and, borne forth by the mighty arm, go crashing through casque, and skull, and brain, invading the Unknown with yet another bewildered ghost. I should like to live in *that* room if I could only get into it."

===Chinese===
第二天他比平日回家早，把镜子固定在墙上，安在长房间一端的壁炉上方。随后他细心拭去镜面上的灰尘，镜子便从那遮蔽着它的妒忌的尘衣之下焕然生光，清澈得像一泓映着阳光的泉水。但他的兴致主要放在镜框那奇特的雕饰上。他先用刷子尽力把它刷净，继而逐处细细审察，盼能发现某种足以揭示雕刻者用心的线索。然而他未能如愿；末了，他略觉倦怠与失望地停了下来，对着镜中房间的幽深处茫然凝望了片刻。但不多时，他半自语般说道：「镜子是件多么奇怪的东西啊！它和人的想象之间，又存在着多么奇妙的亲和！因为我这间房间，我在镜中看它时，是同一间，却又不是同一间。它并非我住的这间房间的单纯映像，倒像是我正在一篇自己喜欢的小说里读到它。它那平凡的气息全然消失了。镜子把它从事实的领域提升到艺术的国度；而它被这样映现给我这一点，便已给那原本生硬赤裸的东西披上了兴味；正如人在台上欣喜地看着某个角色的扮演，而在生活里，人却会对那角色避之唯恐不及，像躲避某种不堪忍受的单调乏味。可是，这岂不更是因为艺术把自然从我们感官那倦怠餍足的注视中抢救出来，从我们焦灼的日常生活那贬抑人的不公中抢救出来，转而向那离群独处的想象力发出呼唤，从而在某种程度上显出自然的本来面目，显出她呈现在孩子眼中的模样——孩子的日常生活无所畏惧亦无所奢求，迎面撞见这满溢奇迹的世界真正的意涵，便毫不追问地欢欣喜乐？就说那具骷髅吧——我几乎有些怕它，它立在那里那么安静，眼睛只望着看不见的事物，像一座瞭望塔，越过这忙碌人世的全部荒原，望向彼方安静的安息之境。可它的每一根骨头、每一处关节，我都熟得像自己的拳头一样。还有那柄古战斧，看上去仿佛随时会被一只戴甲的手抓起，借着健臂之力抡将出去，咔嚓一声连破头盔、头骨与脑髓，带着又一个迷迷惘惘的鬼魂，侵扰那未知之境。我倒真愿意住在*那*间房间里，只要我进得去。」

===Original===
Scarcely had the half-moulded words floated from him, as he stood gazing into the mirror, when, striking him as with a flash of amazement that fixed him in his posture, noiseless and unannounced, glided suddenly through the door into the reflected room, with stately motion, yet reluctant and faltering step, the graceful form of a woman, clothed all in white. Her back only was visible as she walked slowly up to the couch in the further end of the room, on which she laid herself wearily, turning towards him a face of unutterable loveliness, in which suffering, and dislike, and a sense of compulsion, strangely mingled with the beauty. He stood without the power of motion for some moments, with his eyes irrecoverably fixed upon her; and even after he was conscious of the ability to move, he could not summon up courage to turn and look on her, face to face, in the veritable chamber in which he stood. At length, with a sudden effort, in which the exercise of the will was so pure, that it seemed involuntary, he turned his face to the couch. It was vacant. In bewilderment, mingled with terror, he turned again to the mirror: there, on the reflected couch, lay the exquisite lady-form. She lay with closed eyes, whence two large tears were just welling from beneath the veiling lids; still as death, save for the convulsive motion of her bosom.

===Chinese===
他正站着凝望镜中，那半成形的话语刚从唇边飘出，忽有一位女子的优雅身影，通体素白，悄无声息、未经宣告，蓦地穿门而入，滑进镜中的房间；她步态雍容，却又迟疑踉跄，仿佛勉为其难——这一景象如一道惊愕的闪电劈来，把他定在了原位。他只看得见她的背影：她缓缓走向房间尽头的长榻，倦怠地躺了上去，把一张美得难以言宣的脸转向他；那张脸上，痛苦、嫌恶与身不由己之感，奇异地与美貌交织在一起。他失了行动之力似的站了半晌，目光钉在她身上，收不回来；甚至在他觉出自己已能行动之后，也鼓不起勇气转过身去，在真实的房间里与她面对面相看。末了，他猛一使劲——那意志的运用如此纯粹，倒像出于无意——把脸转向长榻。榻上空无一物。他在惶惑与恐怖交织之中再看镜子：那里，在镜中的长榻上，躺着那窈窕的女体。她闭着眼躺着，两大颗泪水正从掩覆的睫毛下涌出；静得像死，只有胸口的起伏一阵阵痉挛。

===Original===
Cosmo himself could not have described what he felt. His emotions were of a kind that destroyed consciousness, and could never be clearly recalled. He could not help standing yet by the mirror, and keeping his eyes fixed on the lady, though he was painfully aware of his rudeness, and feared every moment that she would open hers, and meet his fixed regard. But he was, ere long, a little relieved; for, after a while, her eyelids slowly rose, and her eyes remained uncovered, but unemployed for a time; and when, at length, they began to wander about the room, as if languidly seeking to make some acquaintance with her environment, they were never directed towards him: it seemed nothing but what was in the mirror could affect her vision; and, therefore, if she saw him at all, it could only be his back, which, of necessity, was turned towards her in the glass. The two figures in the mirror could not meet face to face, except he turned and looked at her, present in his room; and, as she was not there, he concluded that if he were to turn towards the part in his room corresponding to that in which she lay, his reflection would either be invisible to her altogether, or at least it must appear to her to gaze vacantly towards her, and no meeting of the eyes would produce the impression of spiritual proximity. By and by her eyes fell upon the skeleton, and he saw her shudder and close them. She did not open them again, but signs of repugnance continued evident on her countenance. Cosmo would have removed the obnoxious thing at once, but he feared to discompose her yet more by the assertion of his presence which the act would involve. So he stood and watched her. The eyelids yet shrouded the eyes, as a costly case the jewels within; the troubled expression gradually faded from the countenance, leaving only a faint sorrow behind; the features settled into an unchanging expression of rest; and by these signs, and the slow regular motion of her breathing, Cosmo knew that she slept. He could now gaze on her without embarrassment. He saw that her figure, dressed in the simplest robe of white, was worthy of her face; and so harmonious, that either the delicately-moulded foot, or any finger of the equally delicate hand, was an index to the whole. As she lay, her whole form manifested the relaxation of perfect repose. He gazed till he was weary, and at last seated himself near the newfound shrine, and mechanically took up a book, like one who watches by a sickbed. But his eyes gathered no thoughts from the page before him. His intellect had been stunned by the bold contradiction, to its face, of all its experience, and now lay passive, without assertion, or speculation, or even conscious astonishment; while his imagination sent one wild dream of blessedness after another coursing through his soul. How long he sat he knew not; but at length he roused himself, rose, and, trembling in every portion of his frame, looked again into the mirror. She was gone. The mirror reflected faithfully what his room presented, and nothing more. It stood there like a golden setting whence the central jewel has been stolen away; like a night-sky without the glory of its stars. She had carried with her all the strangeness of the reflected room. It had sunk to the level of the one without. But when the first pangs of his disappointment had passed, Cosmo began to comfort himself with the hope that she might return, perhaps the next evening, at the same hour. Resolving that if she did, she should not at least be scared by the hateful skeleton, he removed that and several other articles of questionable appearance into a recess by the side of the hearth, whence they could not possibly cast any reflection into the mirror; and having made his poor room as tidy as he could, sought the solace of the open sky and of a night wind that had begun to blow; for he could not rest where he was. When he returned, somewhat composed, he could hardly prevail with himself to lie down on his bed; for he could not help feeling as if she had lain upon it; and for him to lie there now would be something like sacrilege. However, weariness prevailed; and laying himself on the couch, dressed as he was, he slept till day.

===Chinese===
科斯莫自己也说不出他感觉到的是什么。他的情感属于那种足以摧毁意识的一类，事后永远无法清晰地追忆。他不由自主地仍站在镜前，眼睛盯住那女子，虽然他痛切地自知失礼，又时时刻刻害怕她会睁开眼来，撞上他这凝注的注视。但不久他稍稍宽了心；因为过了一会儿，她的眼睑缓缓抬起，双眼袒露出来，却一时无所视物；及至那双眼睛终于开始在房间里逡巡，仿佛倦怠地想认一认四围的环境，却始终不曾朝向他：似乎唯有镜中之物才能进入她的视觉；因此，她若真能看见他，也只能看见他的背影——他在镜中的像必然背对着她。镜中的两个身影无法面对面相会，除非他转过身去，看她那就在自己房间里的真身；而她并不在这里，于是他断定，倘若他转向自己房间里与她所卧之处对应的那一部分，他的映像要么她全然看不见，要么在她看来只是在空茫地朝她的方向呆望，不会有目光的相遇，产生不出心灵相近的印象。过了一会儿，她的目光落到那具骷髅上，他看见她一颤，闭上了眼睛。她再没有睁开，但嫌恶之色一直明明白白挂在脸上。科斯莫本想立刻把那可憎的东西搬走，却又怕这个动作势必要宣示自己在场，反而搅得她更加不安。于是他站着看她。眼睑仍遮着双眼，像贵重的匣子覆着其中的珠宝；脸上的纷乱之色渐渐消退，只余一缕淡淡的哀愁；容貌凝定成一种不变的安息神情；凭着这些征象，加上她呼吸迟缓均匀的起伏，科斯莫知道她睡着了。此刻他尽可以端详她而不觉窘迫。他看出她的身段——穿着一身最素净的白袍——配得上她的脸庞；处处匀停和谐，连那纤秀的足，或那同样纤秀的手上的任何一根手指，都是全体的写照。她躺着的时候，整个形体都显出完美安歇的松弛。他望到眼睛发倦，末了在这新得的圣龛近旁坐下，机械地拿起一本书，像个守在病榻前的人。但眼前的书页没能给他的心聚起任何思想。他的理智已被那当面的、大胆的驳斥——驳斥它全部的经验——打得晕头转向，此刻只能被动地躺着，不作主张，不作推究，甚至没有清醒的惊愕；他的想象却把一个个狂野的极乐之梦接二连三地放出去，在他灵魂里奔驰。他坐了多久，自己也不知道；末了他猛然回神，站起身来，浑身每一部分都在颤抖，再朝镜中望去。她不见了。镜子忠实地映出他房间的原样，别无所有。它立在那里，像一座金质的托座，中央的宝石已被窃走；像一片失去了星光荣耀的夜空。她把镜中房间的全部奇异都随身带走了。它已跌落回外间那同一个水平。但当失望最初的剧痛过去之后，科斯莫开始用一个希望安慰自己：她也许还会回来，也许就在明晚，同一个时辰。他打定主意，她若再来，至少不能让那可憎的骷髅吓着她，便把它和另外几件模样可疑的东西搬进壁炉侧旁的一个凹壁里，从那里断然照不进镜子；又把可怜的房间尽量收拾整洁，便出门去，向开阔的天空和一阵已经刮起的夜风寻求慰藉；因为他在原地实在安坐不住。等他回来，心境稍定，却几乎说服不了自己在床上躺下；他禁不住觉得她仿佛在上面躺过；此刻他若躺上去，就近乎亵渎了。然而倦意毕竟占了上风；他衣服也不脱，就在躺榻上睡下，一觉直到天明。

===Original===
With a beating heart, beating till he could hardly breathe, he stood in dumb hope before the mirror, on the following evening. Again the reflected room shone as through a purple vapor in the gathering twilight. Everything seemed waiting like himself for a coming splendor to glorify its poor earthliness with the presence of a heavenly joy. And just as the room vibrated with the strokes of the neighboring church bell, announcing the hour of six, in glided the pale beauty, and again laid herself on the couch. Poor Cosmo nearly lost his senses with delight. She was there once more! Her eyes sought the corner where the skeleton had stood, and a faint gleam of satisfaction crossed her face, apparently at seeing it empty. She looked suffering still, but there was less of discomfort expressed in her countenance than there had been the night before. She took more notice of the things about her, and seemed to gaze with some curiosity on the strange apparatus standing here and there in her room. At length, however, drowsiness seemed to overtake her, and again she fell asleep. Resolved not to lose sight of her this time, Cosmo watched the sleeping form. Her slumber was so deep and absorbing, that a fascinating repose seemed to pass contagiously from her to him as he gazed upon her; and he started as if from a dream, when the lady moved, and, without opening her eyes, rose, and passed from the room with the gait of a somnambulist.

===Chinese===
第二天傍晚，他怀着一颗怦怦直跳的心——跳得几乎喘不过气来——站在镜前，怀着无声的希望。镜中的房间又一次亮起来，透过渐浓的暮色，仿佛隔着一层紫色的岚气。一切都像他自己一样在等候，等一片将临的辉光以天国般的欢愉降临，圣化它那可怜的尘俗。恰在邻近教堂的钟声报出六点、余音震得房间嗡嗡作响之时，那位苍白的美人悄悄滑了进来，又在长榻上躺下。可怜的科斯莫欢喜得几乎失了神智。她又在这里了！她的目光寻找骷髅先前立着的那一角，显然因为看见那里空了，脸上一丝满意的光微微掠过。她看上去仍有痛苦，但面容上显出的不适已比前夜为少。她更多留意起四围的东西，似乎带着几分好奇，打量她房间里东一处西一处立着的古怪器具。然而末了，睡意仿佛袭来，她又一次睡着了。这回他打定主意不再让目光离开她，科斯莫守着那睡着的身形。她的睡眠那样深沉、那样摄人，一种迷人的安恬仿佛带着传染性，从他所凝望的她身上传到他身上；忽然那女子动了，眼睛也不睁，站起身，以梦游者的步态走出了房间，他这才如从梦中一惊。

===Original===
Cosmo was now in a state of extravagant delight. Most men have a secret treasure somewhere. The miser has his golden hoard; the virtuoso his pet ring; the student his rare book; the poet his favorite haunt; the lover his secret drawer; but Cosmo had a mirror with a lovely lady in it. And now that he knew by the skeleton, that she was affected by the things around her, he had a new object in life: he would turn the bare chamber in the mirror into a room such as no lady need disdain to call her own. This he could effect only by furnishing and adorning his. And Cosmo was poor. Yet he possessed accomplishments that could be turned to account; although, hitherto, he had preferred living on his slender allowance, to increasing his means by what his pride considered unworthy of his rank. He was the best swordsman in the University; and now he offered to give lessons in fencing and similar exercises, to such as chose to pay him well for the trouble. His proposal was heard with surprise by the students; but it was eagerly accepted by many; and soon his instructions were not confined to the richer students, but were anxiously sought by many of the young nobility of Prague and its neighborhood. So that very soon he had a good deal of money at his command. The first thing he did was to remove his apparatus and oddities into a closet in the room. Then he placed his bed and a few other necessaries on each side of the hearth, and parted them from the rest of the room by two screens of Indian fabric. Then he put an elegant couch for the lady to lie upon, in the corner where his bed had formerly stood; and, by degrees, every day adding some article of luxury, converted it, at length, into a rich boudoir.

===Chinese===
科斯莫此刻处于一种过分的欢愉之中。多数人总在什么地方藏着一件秘宝。守财奴有他的金窖；鉴赏家有他的爱戒；书生有他的珍本；诗人有他的流连之地；恋人有他的暗屉；而科斯莫有一面镜子，里面住着一位可爱的女子。如今他从骷髅一事得知，她会被四围的东西所触动，他的人生便有了一个新的目标：他要把镜中那间光秃秃的斗室，变成一间任凭哪位女士都无须嫌弃、乐得认作自己的房间。而要做到这一点，唯有布置装点他自己的房间。科斯莫是穷的。然而他身有可以变现的本领；只是直到如今，他宁可靠着微薄的津贴过活，也不肯用他那自尊心目中配不上自己身份的手段去开源。他是全大学最好的剑客；如今他提出愿教授剑术和类似的操练，只要有人肯出好价钱酬他的辛劳。学生们听着都觉意外；但许多人热切地领受了；不久他的传授便不限于富裕的学生，布拉格及其近郊的许多年轻贵族也殷殷求教。于是很快，他手头便有了不少钱可以支配。他做的头一件事，是把器具和古怪玩物搬进屋里的一间小壁橱。然后把床和另外几件必需之物安放在壁炉两侧，用两架印度织物的屏风与房间的其余部分隔开。再为那女子置一张雅致的长榻供她安卧，就放在原先摆床的那个角落；又一天添一件奢华的小物，渐渐地，终于把它布置成一间富丽的闺阁。

===Original===
Every night, about the same time, the lady entered. The first time she saw the new couch, she started with a half-smile; then her face grew very sad, the tears came to her eyes, and she laid herself upon the couch, and pressed her face into the silken cushions, as if to hide from everything. She took notice of each addition and each change as the work proceeded; and a look of acknowledgment, as if she knew that someone was ministering to her, and was grateful for it, mingled with the constant look of suffering. At length, after she had lain down as usual one evening, her eyes fell upon some paintings with which Cosmo had just finished adorning the walls. She rose, and to his great delight, walked across the room, and proceeded to examine them carefully, testifying much pleasure in her looks as she did so. But again the sorrowful, tearful expression returned, and again she buried her face in the pillows of her couch. Gradually, however, her countenance had grown more composed; much of the suffering manifest on her first appearance had vanished, and a kind of quiet, hopeful expression had taken its place; which, however, frequently gave way to an anxious, troubled look, mingled with something of sympathetic pity.

===Chinese===
每天夜里，大约同一个时辰，女子进来了。头一回看见那张新长榻，她一怔，露出半分笑意；随即脸色变得十分凄然，泪水涌上眼睛，她在榻上躺下，把脸埋进丝垫里，仿佛要躲开一切。工程进行之中，每一件添置、每一处改动，她都看在眼里；一种领受的神情——仿佛她知道有人在侍奉她，并为此感激——时时与她那惯常的痛苦之色交织在一起。终于，一天傍晚她照例躺下之后，目光落在几幅画上——科斯莫刚把墙壁用它们装点完毕。她起身，踱过房间，逐一细细赏看，神色之间颇见欣悦——这使他欢喜无量。可随后那哀伤的、含泪的神情又回来了，她又把脸埋进长榻的枕头里。然而渐渐地，她的面容变得安宁多了；她初现时那种触目的痛苦大半已然消散，换成了一种静静的、含着希望的神情；只是这神情又常常让位于一种焦灼不安的容色，其中还夹杂着几分同情与怜悯。

===Original===
Meantime, how fared Cosmo? As might be expected in one of his temperament, his interest had blossomed into love, and his love—shall I call it *ripened*, or—*withered* into passion? But, alas! he loved a shadow. He could not come near her, could not speak to her, could not hear a sound from those sweet lips, to which his longing eyes would cling like bees to their honey-founts. Ever and anon, he sang to himself:

> "I shall die for love of the maiden;"

and ever he looked again, and died not, though his heart seemed ready to break with intensity of life and longing. And the more he did for her, the more he loved her; and he hoped that, although she never appeared to see him, yet she was pleased to think that one unknown would give his life to her. He tried to comfort himself over his separation from her, by thinking that perhaps some day she would see him and make signs to him, and that would satisfy him; "for," thought he, "is not this all that a loving soul can do to enter into communion with another? Nay, how many who love, never come nearer than to behold each other as in a mirror; seem to know and yet never know the inward life; never enter the other soul; and part at last, with but the vaguest notion of the universe on the borders of which they have been hovering for years? If I could but speak to her, and knew that she heard me, I should be satisfied." Once he contemplated painting a picture on the wall, which should, of necessity, convey to the lady a thought of himself; but, though he had some skill with the pencil, he found his hand tremble so much when he began the attempt, that he was forced to give it up.

===Chinese===
这期间，科斯莫又如何了呢？正如他那样的性子所能预料的，他的兴致已开放成爱情，而他的爱情——我该说它*成熟*了呢，还是——*枯萎*成了激情？可是，唉！他爱的是一个影子。他无法走近她，无法与她说话，无法从那两片甜甜的唇间听到一点声息——他渴慕的眼睛依恋着那唇，像蜜蜂依恋着蜜的源泉。他时而对着自己唱道：

> 「我将为爱那位姑娘而死；」

唱了又看，看了又不曾死，虽然他的心仿佛要被生命与渴望的烈度涨破。他为她们做得越多，便爱得越深；他抱着一个希望：虽然她似乎从未看见过他，但她想到有一个无名之人肯把生命献给她，总该是欢喜的。他与她隔绝，便这样安慰自己：说不定哪一天她会看见他，向他打手势，那就足以使他满足了；「因为，」他想，「一个有爱的灵魂想与另一个灵魂相通，所能做的，岂非尽在于此？不然你看，多少相爱之人，彼此接近的程度，从不超过隔着一面镜子相望；仿佛认识那内在的生命，却永不认识；从不进入另一个灵魂；到头来分袂了，对于他们多年在其边缘盘桓的那个宇宙，所怀的仍是极其模糊的印象。我若能与她说话，并知道她听见了，我就满足了。」有一回他想在墙上画一幅画，那画必然会把他的心思传达给那女子；但他虽有几分丹青之技，才一开始动笔，手便抖得厉害，只得作罢。

===Original===
One evening, as he stood gazing on his treasure, he thought he saw a faint expression of self-consciousness on her countenance, as if she surmised that passionate eyes were fixed upon her. This grew; till at last the red blood rose over her neck, and cheek, and brow. Cosmo's longing to approach her became almost delirious. This night she was dressed in an evening costume, resplendent with diamonds. This could add nothing to her beauty, but it presented it in a new aspect; enabled her loveliness to make a new manifestation of itself in a new embodiment. For essential beauty is infinite; and, as the soul of Nature needs an endless succession of varied forms to embody her loveliness, countless faces of beauty springing forth, not any two the same, at any one of her heartthrobs; so the individual form needs an infinite change of its environments, to enable it to uncover all the phases of its loveliness. Diamonds glittered from amidst her hair, half hidden in its luxuriance, like stars through dark rain-clouds; and the bracelets on her white arms flashed all the colors of a rainbow of lightnings, as she lifted her snowy hands to cover her burning face. But her beauty shone down all its adornment. "If I might have but one of her feet to kiss," thought Cosmo, "I should be content." Alas! he deceived himself, for passion is never content. Nor did he know that there are *two* ways out of her enchanted house. But, suddenly, as if the pang had been driven into his heart from without, revealing itself first in pain, and afterwards in definite form, the thought darted into his mind, "She has a lover somewhere. Remembered words of his bring the color on her face now. I am nowhere to her. She lives in another world all day, and all night, after she leaves me. Why does she come and make me love her, till I, a strong man, am too faint to look upon her more?" He looked again, and her face was pale as a lily. A sorrowful compassion seemed to rebuke the glitter of the restless jewels, and the slow tears rose in her eyes. She left her room sooner this evening than was her wont. Cosmo remained alone, with a feeling as if his bosom had been suddenly left empty and hollow, and the weight of the whole world was crushing in its walls. The next evening, for the first time since she began to come, she came not.

===Chinese===
一天傍晚，他正站着凝望他的珍宝，仿佛看见她脸上掠过一丝自觉的神情，好像她料到有热恋的眼睛正盯着自己。这神情渐渐滋长；末了，红潮漫上她的颈项、面颊和额头。科斯莫想要接近她的渴望，几乎发为谵狂。这一晚她穿一身晚装，钻石辉煌。这于她的美原不能增色分毫，却把那美换了一个面目呈现出来；使她的可爱得以在一种新的化身里作一次新的显现。因为本质的美是无限的；正如自然的灵魂需要无穷接续的万千形态来体现她的可爱——她每一次心搏，都迸发出无数美的面容，绝无两个相同——个体的形骸也同样需要环境作无穷的变化，才能把它可爱的诸般面相一一揭展开来。钻石在她的发间闪烁，半掩在浓发丰茂的深处，像黑雨云间透出的星；她举起雪白的手去掩那灼热的脸时，白臂上的钏镯闪出一片闪电之虹的所有颜色。但她的美压倒了这一切妆饰。「倘若能容我吻一吻她的脚，」科斯莫想，「我就知足了。」唉！他骗了自己，因为激情从不知足。他也不知道，出她那被施魔的宅邸，原有*两*条路。可是，蓦地——仿佛那剧痛是从外面钉进他心里的，先显为疼痛，随后才显出确定的形状——一个念头直刺进他脑海：「她在什么地方有一个恋人。如今是她心上人旧日的话语，把她脸上的红潮唤起来的。我在她那里算不了什么。她离开我之后，整天整夜都活在另一个世界里。她为什么来，惹得我爱她，害得我一条壮汉，连再看她一眼都支持不住？」他再看时，她的脸已苍白如一朵百合。一种哀伤的怜悯仿佛在斥责那些躁动不安的珠钻的光芒，泪水缓缓地升上她的眼睛。这天晚上，她离开房间比往常都早。科斯莫独自留下，只觉胸膛忽然空了、瘪了，整个世界的重量正把它的四壁压坍下来。第二天晚上，她没有来——自她开始来之后，这是头一遭。

===Original===
And now Cosmo was in wretched plight. Since the thought of a rival had occurred to him, he could not rest for a moment. More than ever he longed to see the lady face to face. He persuaded himself that if he but knew the worst he would be satisfied; for then he could abandon Prague, and find that relief in constant motion, which is the hope of all active minds when invaded by distress. Meantime he waited with unspeakable anxiety for the next night, hoping she would return: but she did not appear. And now he fell really ill. Rallied by his fellow-students on his wretched looks, he ceased to attend the lectures. His engagements were neglected. He cared for nothing. The sky, with the great sun in it, was to him a heartless, burning desert. The men and women in the streets were mere puppets, without motives in themselves, or interest to him. He saw them all as on the ever-changing field of a camera obscura. She—she alone and altogether—was his universe, his well of life, his incarnate good. For six evenings she came not. Let his absorbing passion, and the slow fever that was consuming his brain, be his excuse for the resolution which he had taken and begun to execute, before that time had expired.

===Chinese===
这下科斯莫的处境凄惨了。自从那情敌的念头生出来，他一刻也不得安息。他比以往任何时候都更渴望与那女子面对面相见。他说服自己：只要知道最坏的结局，也就可以甘心了；因为那时他可以离开布拉格，在不断的奔波中寻得解脱——凡是活跃的心灵被忧患侵入时，都以此自期。同时他以难以言宣的焦灼等着第二天晚上，盼她回来：她却没有出现。于是他真的病倒了。同学们打趣他形容憔悴，他便不再去听讲。该做的事都荒疏了。他什么都无所谓了。那缀着大太阳的天空，在他眼里是一片无情的、燃烧的荒漠。街上的男男女女，不过是些木偶，自己没有动机，与他也不相干。他看他们，就像看暗箱里那片变幻不定的幕布上的影子。她——唯独她，也完完全全地是她——是他的宇宙，他的生命之泉，他化身为肉的美善。一连六个晚上她没有来。就让他那摄魂夺魄的激情，和那正在蚕食他脑力的慢性热病，替他在这期限未满之前便已拿定并且着手施行的那决心，作一番开脱吧。

===Original===
Reasoning with himself, that it must be by some enchantment connected with the mirror, that the form of the lady was to be seen in it, he determined to attempt to turn to account what he had hitherto studied principally from curiosity. "For," said he to himself, "if a spell can force her presence in that glass (and she came unwillingly at first), may not a stronger spell, such as I know, especially with the aid of her half-presence in the mirror, if ever she appears again, compel her living form to come to me here? If I do her wrong, let love be my excuse. I want only to know my doom from her own lips." He never doubted, all the time, that she was a real earthly woman; or, rather, that there was a woman, who, somehow or other, threw this reflection of her form into the magic mirror.

===Chinese===
他这样与自己讲理：那女子之形既然能在镜中出现，必定是靠着某种与镜子相连的魔法的；于是他决意试着把他此前主要出于好奇而研习的学问，拿来派上用场。「因为，」他自忖道，「既然一个咒能够强使她的身影显在那面镜里（何况她起初来时并不情愿），那么一个更强的咒——我正懂得一个——尤其是在她那半个身影现于镜中的时候助上一臂，只要她再出现，岂不能强使她活生生的形体来到我这里？我若错待了她，就让爱替我声辩吧。我只求从她自己口中知道我的命运。」他自始至终毫不怀疑，她是一个真实的人间女子；或者说得更确切些，他毫不怀疑世上有这么一个女子，不知怎的，把自己形体的这一重映像投进了那面魔镜。

===Original===
He opened his secret drawer, took out his books of magic, lighted his lamp, and read and made notes from midnight till three in the morning, for three successive nights. Then he replaced his books; and the next night went out in quest of the materials necessary for the conjuration. These were not easy to find; for, in love-charms and all incantations of this nature, ingredients are employed scarcely fit to be mentioned, and for the thought even of which, in connection with her, he could only excuse himself on the score of his bitter need. At length he succeeded in procuring all he required; and on the seventh evening from that on which she had last appeared, he found himself prepared for the exercise of unlawful and tyrannical power.

===Chinese===
他打开暗屉，取出他的秘术之书，点亮灯，一连三夜，从午夜读到凌晨三点，边读边作札记。随后他把书放回原处；第二天夜里出门，去寻那召唤仪式所必需的材料。这些材料并不易得；因为凡属爱情符咒和这一类的咒法，所用的配料几乎不宜提起，而只要想到把它们与她相联，他就只能以自己苦痛的需要为由，替自己开脱。末了他总算把所需的一切都置办齐了；从她最后一次现身的那一晚算起，到第七个晚上，他发觉自己已经准备好，要去施行那不法的、僭越的威力了。

===Original===
He cleared the center of the room; stooped and drew a circle of red on the floor, around the spot where he stood; wrote in the four quarters mystical signs, and numbers which were all powers of seven or nine; examined the whole ring carefully, to see that no smallest break had occurred in the circumference; and then rose from his bending posture. As he rose, the church clock struck seven; and, just as she had appeared the first time, reluctant, slow, and stately, glided in the lady. Cosmo trembled; and when, turning, she revealed a countenance worn and wan, as with sickness or inward trouble, he grew faint, and felt as if he dared not proceed. But as he gazed on the face and form, which now possessed his whole soul, to the exclusion of all other joys and griefs, the longing to speak to her, to know that she heard him, to hear from her one word in return, became so unendurable, that he suddenly and hastily resumed his preparations. Stepping carefully from the circle, he put a small brazier into its center. He then set fire to its contents of charcoal, and while it burned up, opened his window and seated himself, waiting, beside it.

===Chinese===
他把房间中央腾空；俯下身去，在地上绕着他立着的那一点，画了一个红色的圆圈；在四象方位写下神秘的符记，以及一些数字，无一不是七或九的乘方；再把整个环圈细细验看一遍，确认周沿没有一丝最细的断口；然后从俯曲的姿势直起身来。他起身时，教堂的钟正敲七点；那女子便像她第一次现身时那样，踌躇着、缓缓地、雍容地滑了进来。科斯莫发着抖；当她转过脸来，露出一张憔悴苍白的面容——像缠绵病榻，又像心事重重——他一阵发昏，觉得自己几乎不敢再往下进行了。但他凝望着那张脸、那个身形——如今它们占据了他的整个灵魂，把一切别的欢欣与悲苦都排除在外——想对她说话、想知道她听得见、想从她那里听到一句回答的渴望，变得这样难以忍受，以致他忽然慌慌张张地重新动手准备起来。他小心地跨出圈外，把一个小火盆放进圈心。然后点燃盆中的木炭，趁它烧旺的当儿，推开窗子，在火盆旁边坐下来等着。

===Original===
It was a sultry evening. The air was full of thunder. A sense of luxurious depression filled the brain. The sky seemed to have grown heavy, and to compress the air beneath it. A kind of purplish tinge pervaded the atmosphere, and through the open window came the scents of the distant fields, which all the vapors of the city could not quench. Soon the charcoal glowed. Cosmo sprinkled upon it the incense and other substances which he had compounded, and, stepping within the circle, turned his face from the brazier and towards the mirror. Then, fixing his eyes upon the face of the lady, he began with a trembling voice to repeat a powerful incantation. He had not gone far, before the lady grew pale; and then, like a returning wave, the blood washed all its banks with its crimson tide, and she hid her face in her hands. Then he passed to a conjuration stronger yet. The lady rose and walked uneasily to and fro in her room. Another spell; and she seemed seeking with her eyes for some object on which they wished to rest. At length it seemed as if she suddenly espied him; for her eyes fixed themselves full and wide upon his, and she drew gradually, and somewhat unwillingly, close to her side of the mirror, just as if his eyes had fascinated her. Cosmo had never seen her so near before. Now at least, eyes met eyes; but he could not quite understand the expression of hers. They were full of tender entreaty, but there was something more that he could not interpret. Though his heart seemed to labor in his throat, he would allow no delight or agitation to turn him from his task. Looking still in her face, he passed on to the mightiest charm he knew. Suddenly the lady turned and walked out of the door of her reflected chamber. A moment after she entered his room with veritable presence; and, forgetting all his precautions, he sprang from the charmed circle, and knelt before her. There she stood, the living lady of his passionate visions, alone beside him, in a thundery twilight, and the glow of a magic fire.

===Chinese===
那是一个闷热的夜晚。空气里满是雷意。头脑中充满一种沉甸甸的、近乎逸乐的压抑。天空仿佛变重了，把底下的空气也压紧了。大气里弥漫着一种紫色的色调，敞开的窗口飘进远处田野的气息，全城的雾霭都湮灭不了它。不久木炭发出红光。科斯莫把调合好的香和其他物料洒在炭上，然后走进圈内，把脸从火盆转开，朝着镜子。于是，他盯住那女子的脸，用颤抖的声音开始念诵一篇威力强大的咒文。还没念到多少，那女子的脸就白了；随即，像一道回卷的浪，血液又以绯红的潮水漫过它所有的堤岸，她把脸埋进手里。接着他改用一篇更强的召唤咒。那女子站起身，在房间里不安地走来走去。又是一咒；她仿佛在用眼睛寻找什么对象，好让目光在它上面停驻。末了，她仿佛蓦地瞥见了他；因为她的双眼圆睁，紧紧盯住他的眼睛，一点一点地、颇不情愿地凑近她那一边的镜子，就像他的眼睛把她迷住了似的。科斯莫从来没有离她这样近地看过她。这下至少是眼对眼了；可她眼里的神情，他不能全懂。那里面满是温柔的哀恳，却还有更多的东西，他无从索解。他的心虽然在喉咙里挣动，却不许任何欢欣或慌乱把自己从法事上引开。他依然望着她的脸，把他所知的最强的符咒一路施展开去。忽然那女子转过身，走出她那镜中斗室的门。片刻之后，她以实实在在的临在走进了他的房间；他把一切戒备都忘得干干净净，从法圈里一跃而出，跪倒在她面前。她就立在那里——他炽热的幻梦中有血有肉的女主人公，独自伴着他，在雷声隐隐的暮色里，在魔法之火的红光中。

===Original===
"Why," said the lady, with a trembling voice, "didst thou bring a poor maiden through the rainy streets alone?"

"Because I am dying for love of thee; but I only brought thee from the mirror there."

"Ah, the mirror!" and she looked up at it, and shuddered. "Alas! I am but a slave, while that mirror exists. But do not think it was the power of thy spells that drew me; it was thy longing desire to see me, that beat at the door of my heart, till I was forced to yield."

"Canst thou love me then?" said Cosmo, in a voice calm as death, but almost inarticulate with emotion.

"I do not know," she replied sadly; "that I cannot tell, so long as I am bewildered with enchantments. It were indeed a joy too great, to lay my head on thy bosom and weep to death; for I think thou lovest me, though I do not know;—but—"

Cosmo rose from his knees.

"I love thee as—nay, I know not what—for since I have loved thee, there is nothing else."

He seized her hand: she withdrew it.

"No, better not; I am in thy power, and therefore I may not."

She burst into tears, and kneeling before him in her turn, said—

"Cosmo, if thou lovest me, set me free, even from thyself; break the mirror."

"And shall I see thyself instead?"

"That I cannot tell, I will not deceive thee; we may never meet again."

===Chinese===
「为什么，」女子声音发颤，「为什么你让一个可怜的姑娘独自穿过落雨的街道？」

「因为我正为你爱得要死；不过我只是把你从那面镜子里接来。」

「啊，那镜子！」她抬头望了它一眼，打了个寒噤。「唉！只要那面镜子还在，我就不过是个奴隶。可你别以为是你的咒语有力量把我拘来；是你那想见我一面的热望，敲打着我的心门，敲得我不得不依从。」

「那么你能爱我吗？」科斯莫说，声音平静得像死，却激动得几乎吐不出字来。

「我不知道，」她凄然答道；「这个我说不上，只要我还被魔法搅得神魂颠倒。把我的头枕在你的胸前，一直哭到死，那实在是太大的欢乐；因为我想你是爱我的，虽然我说不准；——可是——」

科斯莫从跪处立起身。

「我爱你如同——不，我不知道如同什么——因为自从爱上你，世上就再没有别的了。」

他一把抓住她的手：她抽了回去。

「不，还是不要；我在你的掌握之中，正因如此，我不能。」

她泪如泉涌，转而跪倒在他面前，说道——

「科斯莫，你若爱我，就还我自由，连你自己也一并放开；把镜子砸了吧。」

「那么我就能见到你本人，作为替代么？」

「这个我说不上，我不愿骗你；我们或许从此不能再相见。」

===Original===
A fierce struggle arose in Cosmo's bosom. Now she was in his power. She did not dislike him at least; and he could see her when he would. To break the mirror would be to destroy his very life to banish out of his universe the only glory it possessed. The whole world would be but a prison, if he annihilated the one window that looked into the paradise of love. Not yet pure in love, he hesitated.

With a wail of sorrow, the lady rose to her feet. "Ah! he loves me not; he loves me not even as I love him; and alas! I care more for his love than even for the freedom I ask."

"I will not wait to be willing," cried Cosmo; and sprang to the corner where the great sword stood.

Meantime it had grown very dark; only the embers cast a red glow through the room. He seized the sword by the steel scabbard, and stood before the mirror; but as he heaved a great blow at it with the heavy pommel, the blade slipped halfway out of the scabbard, and the pommel struck the wall above the mirror. At that moment, a terrible clap of thunder seemed to burst in the very room beside them; and ere Cosmo could repeat the blow, he fell senseless on the hearth. When he came to himself, he found that the lady and the mirror had both disappeared. He was seized with a brain fever, which kept him to his couch for weeks.

===Chinese===
一场猛烈的交战在科斯莫胸中掀起。如今她在他掌握之中。至少她并不厌恶他；他随时想看她就能看到。砸了镜子，就等于毁掉他自己的性命，把他的宇宙里仅有的一点荣光也驱逐出去。倘若他把那扇望进爱的乐园的唯一的窗毁掉，整个世界便只剩下一座监狱。他的爱尚未纯洁，他踌躇了。

女子悲叹一声，立起身来。「唉！他不爱我；他爱我，还不及我爱他；而可叹啊！他这份爱，竟比我所求的自由更教我在意。」

「我不等自己情愿了，」科斯莫喊道，一纵身扑向那柄巨剑竖立的墙角。

这时屋里已经黑透了；只有余烬把一片红光映满房间。他攥住剑的钢鞘，站到镜子跟前；可当他抡起沉重的剑柄圆头朝镜子猛砸下去时，剑身滑出鞘一半，剑柄圆头打在镜子之上的墙上。就在那一刻，一声可怕的霹雳仿佛就在他们身旁的屋子里炸开；科斯莫还没来得及砸第二下，便不省人事，倒在炉床上。待他醒转，发现女子和镜子都不见了。他害了一场脑热病，在躺榻上缠绵了几个星期。

===Original===
When he recovered his reason, he began to think what could have become of the mirror. For the lady, he hoped she had found her way back as she came; but as the mirror involved her fate with its own, he was more immediately anxious about that. He could not think she had carried it away. It was much too heavy, even if it had not been too firmly fixed in the wall, for her to remove it. Then again, he remembered the thunder; which made him believe that it was not the lightning, but some other blow that had struck him down. He concluded that, either by supernatural agency, he having exposed himself to the vengeance of the demons in leaving the circle of safety, or in some other mode, the mirror had probably found its way back to its former owner; and, horrible to think of, might have been by this time once more disposed of, delivering up the lady into the power of another man, who, if he used his power no worse than he himself had done, might yet give Cosmo abundant cause to curse the selfish indecision which prevented him from shattering the mirror at once. Indeed, to think that she whom he loved, and who had prayed to him for freedom, should be still at the mercy, in some degree, of the possessor of the mirror, and was at least exposed to his constant observation, was in itself enough to madden a chary lover.

===Chinese===
神智复原之后，他开始思量那镜子的下落。至于那女子，他盼望她已循着来路回去了；但镜子的命运既与她的命运纠结为一，他眼下更为焦心的倒是镜子。他不能设想是她把它带走的。那镜子重得很，纵然不曾牢牢钉在墙上，也绝不是她的膂力挪得动的。再说，他又记起那声霹雳；这使他相信，把他击倒的不是闪电，而是另外的一击。他断定，或是由于神通的作用——他擅离了那圈安全之界，把自己暴露在群魔的报复之下——或是经由别的什么途径，那镜子多半已经回到了原先的主人手里；而一想就叫人毛骨悚然的是，它此刻或许已经再度脱手，把那女子交到另一个男人的权力之下；那人纵然行使这权力并不比他自己更坏，也尽可以让科斯莫有无穷的理由，去咒诅那份自私的犹豫不决——正是它拦住了他，不让他当时就把镜子砸个粉碎。的确，想到他心爱的人、那个曾哀求他还她自由的人，竟仍然多少握在镜子主人手里，任凭摆布，至少还时时刻刻暴露在那人的窥视之下，单是这一层，就足以使一个情爱专笃的人发狂。

===Original===
Anxiety to be well retarded his recovery; but at length he was able to creep abroad. He first made his way to the old broker's, pretending to be in search of something else. A laughing sneer on the creature's face convinced him that he knew all about it; but he could not see it amongst his furniture, or get any information out of him as to what had become of it. He expressed the utmost surprise at hearing it had been stolen, a surprise which Cosmo saw at once to be counterfeited; while, at the same time, he fancied that the old wretch was not at all anxious to have it mistaken for genuine. Full of distress, which he concealed as well as he could, he made many searches, but with no avail. Of course he could ask no questions; but he kept his ears awake for any remotest hint that might set him in a direction of search. He never went out without a short heavy hammer of steel about him, that he might shatter the mirror the moment he was made happy by the sight of his lost treasure, if ever that blessed moment should arrive. Whether he should see the lady again, was now a thought altogether secondary, and postponed to the achievement of her freedom. He wandered here and there, like an anxious ghost, pale and haggard; gnawed ever at the heart, by the thought of what she might be suffering—all from his fault.

===Chinese===
急于痊愈的心情反倒拖慢了他的康复；但他终于能够出门蹒跚走动了。他先去那老掮客的铺子，佯装寻找别的东西。那老东西脸上一丝带笑的讥诮使他确信：此事他全然知情；可他在满屋的家具里找不见那面镜子，也从他嘴里问不出它的下落。听说镜子被窃，他表示出十二分的惊讶，而科斯莫一眼便看出那是假装的；同时他又觉得，这老坏蛋并不怎么急着让人把别的东西错当成那真货。他满心苦痛，却尽力遮掩着，四下寻访了许多地方，一无所获。他自然什么也不能问；只是支着耳朵，等待哪怕最飘忽的一丝风声，好给自己的搜寻指出一个方向。他出门从不忘记随身带一柄短而沉的钢锤，以便那有福的时刻一旦来临、他得见他失落的珍宝之时，立时把镜子砸个粉碎。能否再见到那女子，如今已是全然居次的念头，退居于成全她的自由之后。他东游西荡，像个忧心忡忡的幽灵，面色灰白，形容枯槁；一个念头总在啮他的心：她此刻不知在受什么苦——而这一切，全是他一手之过。

===Original===
One night, he mingled with a crowd that filled the rooms of one of the most distinguished mansions in the city; for he accepted every invitation, that he might lose no chance, however poor, of obtaining some information that might expedite his discovery. Here he wandered about, listening to every stray word that he could catch, in the hope of a revelation. As he approached some ladies who were talking quietly in a corner, one said to another: "Have you heard of the strange illness of the Princess von Hohenweiss?"

"Yes; she has been ill for more than a year now. It is very sad for so fine a creature to have such a terrible malady. She was better for some weeks lately, but within the last few days the same attacks have returned, apparently accompanied with more suffering than ever. It is altogether an inexplicable story."

"Is there a story connected with her illness?"

"I have only heard imperfect reports of it; but it is said that she gave offence some eighteen months ago to an old woman who had held an office of trust in the family, and who, after some incoherent threats, disappeared. This peculiar affection followed soon after. But the strangest part of the story is its association with the loss of an antique mirror, which stood in her dressing-room, and of which she constantly made use."

Here the speaker's voice sank to a whisper; and Cosmo, although his very soul sat listening in his ears, could hear no more. He trembled too much to dare to address the ladies, even if it had been advisable to expose himself to their curiosity. The name of the Princess was well known to him, but he had never seen her; except indeed it was she, which now he hardly doubted, who had knelt before him on that dreadful night. Fearful of attracting attention, for, from the weak state of his health, he could not recover an appearance of calmness, he made his way to the open air, and reached his lodgings; glad in this, that he at least knew where she lived, although he never dreamed of approaching her openly, even if he should be happy enough to free her from her hateful bondage. He hoped, too, that as he had unexpectedly learned so much, the other and far more important part might be revealed to him ere long.

===Chinese===
一天夜里，他混进了挤满城中一座最显赫府邸厅堂的人群；因为不论什么邀请他都接受，好不错过任何机会——不管多么渺茫——去获得一点可以加速他搜寻的消息。他在那里游来荡去，凡能捕捉到的片言只语无不侧耳以听，盼着一桩水落石出。当他走近几位在角落里低声交谈的女士时，只听一个对另一个说道：「你听说冯·霍恩魏斯公主（Princess von Hohenweiss）得的怪病了吗？」

「听说了；她病到如今已经一年多了。这样一位佳人偏染上这样可怕的病症，实在令人难过。近来有几个星期她好了一些，可最近这几天，同样的发作又回来了，显然比以往任何一次都更痛苦。这整个是一桩无法解释的事。」

「她的病还有什么故事牵连在内吗？」

「我也只听到些残缺不全的传闻；据说约莫十八个月之前，她得罪了一个在府里担任亲信职司的老妇；那老妇语无伦次地恐吓了几句，便失踪了。这种奇特的病症随后就来了。而这故事里最奇怪的一节，是它同一面古镜的失窃牵连在一起：那面镜子原立在她的梳妆室里，她时常使用。」

说到这里，说话人的嗓音低成了耳语；科斯莫虽然整个灵魂都端坐在耳朵里听着，却再也听不见更多了。他抖得太厉害，不敢上前向那几位女士搭话，纵然让自己暴露在她们的好奇心之下原本也无甚妨碍。公主的名字他久已熟悉，却从未见过她本人；除非——如今他已几乎不再怀疑——那夜跪在他面前的正是她。他生怕惹人注意，因为他病体虚弱，怎么也恢复不出一点平静的神色，便抽身走到露天里，回到寓所；他高兴的是这一点：他至少知道了她住在哪里，虽然他连梦也不曾梦想要公开去接近她，哪怕他有幸把她从那可憎的枷锁中解救出来。他还抱着一个希望：他既意外得知了这许多，那另外的、远为紧要的一半，或许不久也会向他显明。

---

===Original===
"Have you seen Steinwald lately?"

"No, I have not seen him for some time. He is almost a match for me at the rapier, and I suppose he thinks he needs no more lessons."

"I wonder what has become of him. I want to see him very much. Let me see; the last time I saw him he was coming out of that old broker's den, to which, if you remember, you accompanied me once, to look at some armor. That is fully three weeks ago."

This hint was enough for Cosmo. Von Steinwald was a man of influence in the court, well known for his reckless habits and fierce passions. The very possibility that the mirror should be in his possession was hell itself to Cosmo. But violent or hasty measures of any sort were most unlikely to succeed. All that he wanted was an opportunity of breaking the fatal glass; and to obtain this he must bide his time. He revolved many plans in his mind, but without being able to fix upon any.

At length, one evening, as he was passing the house of Von Steinwald, he saw the windows more than usually brilliant. He watched for a while; and seeing that company began to arrive, hastened home, and dressed as richly as he could, in the hope of mingling with the guests unquestioned; in effecting which, there could be no difficulty for a man of his carriage.

===Chinese===
「你近来见过施泰因瓦尔德（Steinwald）吗？」

「没有，有一阵子没见了。论花剑他差不多可以和我匹敌了，想必他认为不必再来上课了。」

「不知他如今怎么样了。我很想见他。让我想想；我最后一次见他那回，他正从那老掮客的巢穴里出来——你该记得，你陪我到那里去看过一回盔甲。那已是整整三个星期前的事了。」

这句风声对科斯莫已经足够。冯·施泰因瓦尔德（von Steinwald）是朝中有势力的人物，以行事莽撞、性如烈火出名。那镜子哪怕只是有可能落在他手里，对科斯莫来说便已是地狱本身。但任何激烈的、急躁的手段都极难奏效。他要的只是一个砸碎那致命玻璃的机会；为求得这个机会，他必须待时而动。他心里盘算了种种计划，却没有一个能够拿定。

终于，一天傍晚，他路过冯·施泰因瓦尔德的宅邸，看见窗户亮得比平日更盛。他守望了片刻，见宾客开始陆续到来，便急忙回家，把自己尽可能华贵地打扮起来，指望混在宾客中间无人盘诘；以他那样的仪度，要做到这一点并不为难。

---

===Original===
In a lofty, silent chamber, in another part of the city, lay a form more like marble than a living woman. The loveliness of death seemed frozen upon her face, for her lips were rigid, and her eyelids closed. Her long white hands were crossed over her breast, and no breathing disturbed their repose. Beside the dead, men speak in whispers, as if the deepest rest of all could be broken by the sound of a living voice. Just so, though the soul was evidently beyond the reach of all intimations from the senses, the two ladies, who sat beside her, spoke in the gentlest tones of subdued sorrow.

"She has lain so for an hour."

"This cannot last long, I fear."

"How much thinner she has grown within the last few weeks! If she would only speak, and explain what she suffers, it would be better for her. I think she has visions in her trances, but nothing can induce her to refer to them when she is awake."

"Does she ever speak in these trances?"

"I have never heard her; but they say she walks sometimes, and once put the whole household in a terrible fright by disappearing for a whole hour, and returning drenched with rain, and almost dead with exhaustion and fright. But even then she would give no account of what had happened."

===Chinese===
在城中另一处，一间高敞而寂静的房间里，躺着一个形体，与其说像活着的女子，不如说更像大理石。死亡的美仿佛冻结在她脸上，因为她的嘴唇僵了，眼睑阖着。她修长白皙的双手交叉在胸前，一丝呼吸也不来搅扰它们的安息。在死者身旁，人们说话都用耳语，仿佛那至深的安息会被一个活人的嗓音惊破。正是如此，虽然那灵魂显然已非一切来自感官的音讯所能及，坐在她身旁的两位女士，却用最轻柔的音调说着话，满含压抑的哀伤。

「她这样躺着已经有一个钟点了。」

「我怕，这样子长不了。」

「最近这几个星期她瘦得多了！她只要肯开口，说说她受的是什么罪，对她就会好些。我想她在恍惚之中是见着幻象的，可等她醒着，任凭什么也引不出她提一个字。」

「她在这种恍惚里可曾说过话？」

「我从未听她说过；不过据说她有时会走动，有一回整个宅子里的人吓坏了：她整整失踪了一个钟点，回来时浑身让雨浇透，又乏又怕，几乎没了性命。可就连那时，她也不肯说出了什么事。」

===Original===
A scarce audible murmur from the yet motionless lips of the lady here startled her attendants. After several ineffectual attempts at articulation, the word "Cosmo!" burst from her. Then she lay still as before; but only for a moment. With a wild cry, she sprang from the couch erect on the floor, flung her arms above her head, with clasped and straining hands, and, her wide eyes flashing with light, called aloud, with a voice exultant as that of a spirit bursting from a sepulchre, "I am free! I am free! I thank thee!" Then she flung herself on the couch, and sobbed; then rose, and paced wildly up and down the room, with gestures of mingled delight and anxiety. Then turning to her motionless attendants—"Quick, Lisa, my cloak and hood!" Then lower—"I must go to him. Make haste, Lisa! You may come with me, if you will."

In another moment they were in the street, hurrying along towards one of the bridges over the Moldau. The moon was near the zenith, and the streets were almost empty. The Princess soon outstripped her attendant, and was halfway over the bridge, before the other reached it.

"Are you free, lady? The mirror is broken: are you free?"

The words were spoken close beside her, as she hurried on. She turned; and there, leaning on the parapet in a recess of the bridge, stood Cosmo, in a splendid dress, but with a white and quivering face.

"Cosmo!—I am free—and thy servant forever. I was coming to you now."

"And I to you, for Death made me bold; but I could get no further. Have I atoned at all? Do I love you a little—truly?"

"Ah, I know now that you love me, my Cosmo; but what do you say about death?"

He did not reply. His hand was pressed against his side. She looked more closely: the blood was welling from between the fingers. She flung her arms around him with a faint bitter wail.

When Lisa came up, she found her mistress kneeling above a wan dead face, which smiled on in the spectral moonbeams.

===Chinese===
这时，那女子依旧一动不动的唇间，透出一声几乎听不见的喃喃，惊动了她的侍女。她试着吐了几次音，都没有成字，忽然一声「科斯莫！」迸了出来。随后她照先前一样静静地躺着；但也只是一会儿。她发出一声狂叫，从长榻上一跃而起，直挺挺立在地板上，双臂高举过头，双手紧握，拼命向上伸着，一双睁大的眼睛闪着光，高声喊道——那声音欢腾激越，像是从坟墓中破土而出的一个灵魂——「我自由了！我自由了！我感谢你！」随后她扑倒在长榻上呜咽；又立起身，在房间里发疯似的来回疾走，动作里欢喜与焦灼搅在一处。然后她转向那两个怔立不动的侍女——「快，莉莎，我的斗篷和风帽！」接着又放低了声音——「我必须到他那里去。快些，莉莎！你愿意的话，可以跟我同去。」

转眼之间她们已到了街上，急急地朝伏尔塔瓦河（Moldau）上的一座桥赶去。月亮将近中天，街上几乎空无一人。公主很快就把侍女甩在后面，等侍女踏上桥面，她已走到了桥心。

「你自由了吗，小姐？镜子砸碎了：你自由了吗？」

话音就在她身旁响起，那时她正急急地赶路。她转过身；就在那里，桥上的一处凹龛里，倚着石栏，站着科斯莫，衣履华贵，脸却惨白，簌簌发抖。

「科斯莫！——我自由了——并且永永远远是你的人。我此刻正是到你这里去的。」

「我也是到你这里去的，因为死亡使我放了胆；只是我再走不动了。我可曾将功折罪？我可有一点儿——真心地——爱你？」

「啊，我如今知道你是爱我的，我的科斯莫；可你说到死亡，这是什么话？」

他没有回答。他的手紧紧压在胁下。她凑近细看：鲜血正从指缝间涌出。她双臂抱住他，发出一声微弱的、哀恸的号泣。

等莉莎赶到，她发现女主人跪在一张灰白的死者的脸旁，那张脸在幽灵似的月光里，兀自含着微笑。

---

===Original===
And now I will say no more about these wondrous volumes; though I could tell many a tale out of them, and could, perhaps, vaguely represent some entrancing thoughts of a deeper kind which I found within them. From many a sultry noon till twilight, did I sit in that grand hall, buried and risen again in these old books. And I trust I have carried away in my soul some of the exhalations of their undying leaves. In after hours of deserved or needful sorrow, portions of what I read there have often come to me again, with an unexpected comforting; which was not fruitless, even though the comfort might seem in itself groundless and vain.

===Chinese===
关于这些神奇的书卷，如今我不想再多说了；虽然我可以从中讲出许多故事，或许还能依稀转达我在里面发现的一些更深的、令人心醉的思想。多少个炎热的正午直到黄昏，我坐在那宏大的厅堂里，在这些旧书中埋葬了又复活。我信，我的灵魂里带走了一些它们那不死的书页间蒸腾的气息。在日后那些应分承当、或势所必需的忧伤时辰，我在那里读到过的片段常常重新来到我心头，带着意想不到的安慰；这安慰并非没有果实，纵使它本身看上去似乎全无根据、子虚乌有。

## XIV / 第十四章

===Original===
> Your gallery
> Have we pass'd through, not without much content
> In many singularities; but we saw not
> That which my daughter came to look upon,
> The statue of her mother.

===Chinese===
> 你们的长廊
> 我们已经走过，不无极大的快慰
> 于其中诸多奇处；但我们不曾见到
> 我女儿此来要瞻望的物事，
> 她母亲的雕像。

===Original===
It seemed to me strange, that all this time I had heard no music in the fairy palace. I was convinced there must be music in it, but that my sense was as yet too gross to receive the influence of those mysterious motions that beget sound. Sometimes I felt sure, from the way the few figures of which I got such transitory glimpses passed me, or glided into vacancy before me, that they were moving to the law of music; and, in fact, several times I fancied for a moment that I heard a few wondrous tones coming I knew not whence. But they did not last long enough to convince me that I had heard them with the bodily sense. Such as they were, however, they took strange liberties with me, causing me to burst suddenly into tears, of which there was no presence to make me ashamed, or casting me into a kind of trance of speechless delight, which, passing as suddenly, left me faint and longing for more.

===Chinese===
我觉得奇怪的是，这么久以来，我在这仙宫里竟没有听到过音乐。我确信宫中必定有音乐，只是我的感官还太粗钝，尚不能领受那些生发出声音的神秘运行的影响。有时我几乎可以肯定：我偶得一瞥的那几个人影，掠过我身边、或滑进我眼前的虚空时，那种行进的样子分明是循着音乐的法则在移动；事实上，有好几回，恍惚间我似乎听见几个奇妙的乐音，不知从何处飘来。但它们消逝得太快，不足以让我确信那肉身的感官果真听见了它们。不过，这样的乐音虽则倏忽，对我却放肆得出奇：忽而使我无端涌泪——好在四下无人，不致羞愧——忽而把我抛进一种无言欢喜的出神；这出神又同样骤然消散，只留下我软弱乏力，渴求更多。

===Original===
Now, on an evening, before I had been a week in the palace, I was wandering through one lighted arcade and corridor after another. At length I arrived, through a door that closed behind me, in another vast hall of the palace. It was filled with a subdued crimson light; by which I saw that slender pillars of black, built close to walls of white marble, rose to a great height, and then, dividing into innumerable divergent arches, supported a roof, like the walls, of white marble, upon which the arches intersected intricately, forming a fretting of black upon the white, like the network of a skeleton-leaf. The floor was black. Between several pairs of the pillars upon every side, the place of the wall behind was occupied by a crimson curtain of thick silk, hanging in heavy and rich folds. Behind each of these curtains burned a powerful light, and these were the sources of the glow that filled the hall. A peculiar delicious odor pervaded the place. As soon as I entered, the old inspiration seemed to return to me, for I felt a strong impulse to sing; or rather, it seemed as if someone else was singing a song in my soul, which wanted to come forth at my lips, imbodied in my breath. But I kept silence; and feeling somewhat overcome by the red light and the perfume, as well as by the emotion within me, and seeing at one end of the hall a great crimson chair, more like a throne than a chair, beside a table of white marble, I went to it, and, throwing myself in it, gave myself up to a succession of images of bewildering beauty, which passed before my inward eye, in a long and occasionally crowded train. Here I sat for hours, I suppose; till, returning somewhat to myself, I saw that the red light had paled away, and felt a cool gentle breath gliding over my forehead. I rose and left the hall with unsteady steps, finding my way with some difficulty to my own chamber, and faintly remembering, as I went, that only in the marble cave, before I found the sleeping statue, had I ever had a similar experience.

===Chinese===
进宫还不到一星期的一天傍晚，我正穿过一座又一座灯火通明的拱廊和长廊，信步漫游。末了，我经过一扇在我身后合上的门，来到宫殿的另一座巨大厅堂。厅中充满一种柔和的深红色光辉；借这光我看见，纤细的黑色柱子紧贴白大理石的墙壁筑起，高高地升上去，随后分作无数四散的拱券，撑住一座和墙一样用白大理石筑成的穹顶；拱券在穹顶上繁复交叠，在白色上织成黑色的雕饰，像一片叶脉网络的枯叶。地面是黑的。四周有几对柱子之间，后面墙壁的位置被一幅深红色的厚绸帷幔占据，沉沉地、华美地打着褶垂下。每一幅帷幔后面都燃着强光，这些便是充满大厅的那片红光的来源。一种奇异的甘美香气弥漫全堂。我一进去，往日的灵感仿佛就回到了我身上，因为我心头涌起强烈的歌唱的冲动；不如说，倒仿佛有别人在我灵魂里唱着一支歌，那歌急于涌到我的唇边，在我的呼吸里获得形体。但我保持着沉默；我感到有些被那红光和香气压倒了，也被内心的情绪压倒了；看见大厅一头摆着一把深红色的大椅，与其说是椅，不如说更像王座，旁边是一张白大理石的桌子，我便走过去，把自己抛进椅中，任凭一连串令人目眩神迷的美妙形象，长长地、时而拥挤地列队经过我内在的眼睛。我想我在这里坐了几个钟头；直到略微回过神来，我看见红光已经淡去，觉得一阵清凉柔和的气息拂过我的额头。我起身离开大厅，脚步不稳，费了些周折才摸回自己的房间；一路恍恍惚惚地记起：只有在大理石岩穴里，在我找到那尊沉睡的石像之前，我才有过类似的经历。

===Original===
After this, I repaired every morning to the same hall; where I sometimes sat in the chair and dreamed deliciously, and sometimes walked up and down over the black floor. Sometimes I acted within myself a whole drama, during one of these perambulations; sometimes walked deliberately through the whole epic of a tale; sometimes ventured to sing a song, though with a shrinking fear of I knew not what. I was astonished at the beauty of my own voice as it rang through the place, or rather crept undulating, like a serpent of sound, along the walls and roof of this superb music-hall. Entrancing verses arose within me as of their own accord, chanting themselves to their own melodies, and requiring no addition of music to satisfy the inward sense. But, ever in the pauses of these, when the singing mood was upon me, I seemed to hear something like the distant sound of multitudes of dancers, and felt as if it was the unheard music, moving their rhythmic motion, that within me blossomed in verse and song. I felt, too, that could I but see the dance, I should, from the harmony of complicated movements, not of the dancers in relation to each other merely, but of each dancer individually in the manifested plastic power that moved the consenting harmonious form, understand the whole of the music on the billows of which they floated and swung.

===Chinese===
从那以后，我每天早晨都到那座厅堂去；有时坐在那椅子里做甜美的梦，有时在黑色的地面上走来走去。有时在一次次踱步之间，我在心里演完一整出戏；有时从容地走完一个故事的整部史诗；有时大着胆子唱一支歌，虽然心里怀着一种退缩的畏惧，怕我自己也说不上来的什么。我的嗓音在这殿堂里回荡时——不如说是像一条声音的蛇，起伏蜿蜒地爬过这座华美音乐厅的四壁和穹顶——那声音之美使我自己都惊讶。令人心醉的诗句在我心中自发地涌起，唱着自己的旋律，不需要再添什么音乐来满足内在的感官。但每当歌唱的情绪在我身上，而这些诗句停歇的间隙，我总仿佛听见远处传来大队舞者起舞的声息，并且觉得，正是那未被听见的音乐，推动着她们有节奏的舞步，也在我心中开绽为诗句与歌。我还觉得，倘若我能看见那舞蹈，我便能从繁复动作的和谐之中——不只是舞者彼此之间的协调，更是每一个舞者各自、在那推动着默契和谐之形体的显现出来的塑造伟力之中的和谐——听懂托着她们浮动、摇曳的整片音乐的波涛。

===Original===
At length, one night, suddenly, when this feeling of dancing came upon me, I bethought me of lifting one of the crimson curtains, and looking if, perchance, behind it there might not be hid some other mystery, which might at least remove a step further the bewilderment of the present one. Nor was I altogether disappointed. I walked to one of the magnificent draperies, lifted a corner, and peeped in. There, burned a great, crimson, globe-shaped light, high in the cubical center of another hall, which might be larger or less than that in which I stood, for its dimensions were not easily perceived, seeing that floor and roof and walls were entirely of black marble. The roof was supported by the same arrangement of pillars radiating in arches, as that of the first hall; only, here, the pillars and arches were of dark red. But what absorbed my delighted gaze, was an innumerable assembly of white marble statues, of every form, and in multitudinous posture, filling the hall throughout. These stood, in the ruddy glow of the great lamp, upon pedestals of jet black. Around the lamp shone in golden letters, plainly legible from where I stood, the two words—

> Touch Not!

===Chinese===
终于，一天夜里，这舞蹈之感忽然临到我，我想起去掀开一幅深红色的帷幔，看看它后面是否也许藏着别的什么奥秘，至少能把眼前这层困惑再推进一步。我并没有完全失望。我走到一幅华丽的帷幔前，掀起一角，朝里窥看。那里，在另一座厅堂立方体的中央高处，燃着一团深红的球形巨光；那厅堂比我站的地方或大或小，很难分辨，因为地面、穹顶和四壁全是黑大理石。穹顶由同样的柱子放射成拱的结构支撑着，与第一座厅堂相同；只是这里，柱子和拱券是暗红色的。但吸住我欣喜的凝望的，是无数白大理石雕像的聚会：形形色色，姿态万方，充满了整个厅堂。它们立在那盏巨灯的红光里，各自立在漆黑的基座上。灯的四周，有两个金色的字灿然发光，从我站的地方清晰可读——

> 勿触！

===Original===
There was in all this, however, no solution to the sound of dancing; and now I was aware that the influence on my mind had ceased. I did not go in that evening, for I was weary and faint, but I hoarded up the expectation of entering, as of a great coming joy.

Next night I walked, as on the preceding, through the hall. My mind was filled with pictures and songs, and therewith so much absorbed, that I did not for some time think of looking within the curtain I had last night lifted. When the thought of doing so occurred to me first, I happened to be within a few yards of it. I became conscious at the same moment, that the sound of dancing had been for some time in my ears. I approached the curtain quickly, and, lifting it, entered the black hall. Everything was still as death. I should have concluded that the sound must have proceeded from some other more distant quarter, which conclusion its faintness would, in ordinary circumstances, have necessitated from the first; but there was a something about the statues that caused me still to remain in doubt. As I said, each stood perfectly still upon its black pedestal: but there was about every one a certain air, not of motion, but as if it had just ceased from movement; as if the rest were not altogether of the marbly stillness of thousands of years. It was as if the peculiar atmosphere of each had yet a kind of invisible tremulousness; as if its agitated wavelets had not yet subsided into a perfect calm. I had the suspicion that they had anticipated my appearance, and had sprung, each, from the living joy of the dance, to the death-silence and blackness of its isolated pedestal, just before I entered. I walked across the central hall to the curtain opposite the one I had lifted, and, entering there, found all the appearances similar; only that the statues were different, and differently grouped. Neither did they produce on my mind that impression—of motion just expired, which I had experienced from the others. I found that behind every one of the crimson curtains was a similar hall, similarly lighted, and similarly occupied.

===Chinese===
然而，这一切并没有解开舞蹈之声的谜；而此刻我觉察到，那施加在我心灵上的影响已经停止了。那天傍晚我没有进去，因为我又倦又乏；但我把这进去的期盼珍藏起来，当作一件将临的大欢喜。

第二天夜里，我像前一晚一样穿过大厅。我心里满是图画与歌，沉溺其中，一时间竟没有想起去掀看昨晚掀过的那幅帷幔。等我头一遭想到这事时，恰好已离它只有几步远了。同一刻我也意识到，那舞蹈的声音已在耳畔萦绕了一阵子。我快步走近帷幔，掀起来，走进那黑色的厅堂。一切都死一般寂静。照常理说，那声音那样微弱，我本该从一开始就断定它必是从别的更远的地方传来的；但那些石像身上有一种说不出的什么，使我依然疑虑不定。如我所说，每一尊都一动不动立在黑色基座上：但每一尊身上都有某种气韵，不是动感，而是仿佛刚停止了运动；仿佛那静息并不完全是千年石头的静谧。仿佛每一尊周身的独特气息里，还留有一种看不见的微颤；仿佛它激荡的涟漪尚未平复成全然的宁静。我疑心她们是料到我要来了，便各自在舞的生之欢喜中一跃，跳进那孤绝基座的死寂与黑暗，恰在我进门之前。我穿过中央大厅，走向与我掀开的那幅相对的帷幔，进去一看，种种景象全都相似；只是石像不同，组合的方式也不同。而它们也没有给我那种印象——运动刚刚消逝的感觉——我从头一批石像身上曾经验过这种感觉。我发现每一幅深红帷幔后面都是一座相似的厅堂，同样地燃着光，同样地陈列着石像。

===Original===
The next night, I did not allow my thoughts to be absorbed as before with inward images, but crept stealthily along to the furthest curtain in the hall, from behind which, likewise, I had formerly seemed to hear the sound of dancing. I drew aside its edge as suddenly as I could, and, looking in, saw that the utmost stillness pervaded the vast place. I walked in, and passed through it to the other end. There I found that it communicated with a circular corridor, divided from it only by two rows of red columns. This corridor, which was black, with red niches holding statues, ran entirely about the statue-halls, forming a communication between the further ends of them all; further, that is, as regards the central hall of white whence they all diverged like radii, finding their circumference in the corridor. Round this corridor I now went, entering all the halls, of which there were twelve, and finding them all similarly constructed, but filled with quite various statues, of what seemed both ancient and modern sculpture. After I had simply walked through them, I found myself sufficiently tired to long for rest, and went to my own room.

===Chinese===
再一夜，我不再让自己的心思像先前那样沉溺于内心的形象，而是蹑手蹑脚地溜到大厅最深处的那幅帷幔前——从前我也曾仿佛听见它后面有舞蹈的声音。我用最快的速度猛地拉开帷幔的边角，朝里一望，只见巨大的殿堂里弥漫着极度的寂静。我走进去，一直穿到厅堂另一头。在那里我发现它与一条环形的长廊相通，中间只隔着两排红柱。这条长廊是黑色的，红色的壁龛里立着石像，恰好环绕着所有石像厅堂一整圈，把它们各自的远端连成一气；所谓远端，是相对于那座白色的中央大厅而言——十二座厅堂都从中央大厅像半径一样放射出去，以长廊为它们的圆周。我于是绕着这条长廊走了一圈，把十二座厅堂一一进去看过，发现它们的构造全都相似，只是里面的石像五花八门，像是兼有古代和近代的雕刻。等我单单把它们走完一遍，已经倦得只想休息，便回自己房里去了。

===Original===
In the night I dreamed that, walking close by one of the curtains, I was suddenly seized with the desire to enter, and darted in. This time I was too quick for them. All the statues were in motion, statues no longer, but men and women—all shapes of beauty that ever sprang from the brain of the sculptor, mingled in the convolutions of a complicated dance. Passing through them to the further end, I almost started from my sleep on beholding, not taking part in the dance with the others, nor seemingly endued with life like them, but standing in marble coldness and rigidity upon a black pedestal in the extreme left corner—my lady of the cave; the marble beauty who sprang from her tomb or her cradle at the call of my songs. While I gazed in speechless astonishment and admiration, a dark shadow, descending from above like the curtain of a stage, gradually hid her entirely from my view. I felt with a shudder that this shadow was perchance my missing demon, whom I had not seen for days. I awoke with a stifled cry.

===Chinese===
夜里我梦见，走到一幅帷幔近旁时，进去的欲望忽然攫住我，我便一跃而入。这一回我快得叫她们来不及。所有的石像都在动，已不再是石像，而是男男女女——从雕刻家脑中涌现过的一切美的形骸，交织在一支繁复舞蹈的回旋之中。我穿过人群向厅堂深处走去，忽然几乎从睡梦中惊起：我看见，在极左的角落里，一尊石像既没有加入旁人的舞蹈，也不像她们那样似乎被赋予了生命，而是带着大理石的冰冷僵硬立在一方黑色基座上——那是我的岩穴女郎；那位一听我的歌声呼唤，便从坟墓或摇篮里跃出来的大理石美人。正当我在无言的惊愕与赞叹中凝望时，一道黑影自上方垂落，像舞台的幕布一般，渐渐把她从我眼前完全遮没。我打了个寒噤，觉出这影子兴许正是我失踪的魔影，我已有好些天没见到它了。我带着一声哽住的叫喊醒来。

===Original===
Of course, the next evening I began my journey through the halls (for I knew not to which my dream had carried me), in the hope of proving the dream to be a true one, by discovering my marble beauty upon her black pedestal. At length, on reaching the tenth hall, I thought I recognised some of the forms I had seen dancing in my dream; and to my bewilderment, when I arrived at the extreme corner on the left, there stood, the only one I had yet seen, a vacant pedestal. It was exactly in the position occupied, in my dream, by the pedestal on which the white lady stood. Hope beat violently in my heart.

"Now," said I to myself, "if yet another part of the dream would but come true, and I should succeed in surprising these forms in their nightly dance; it might be the rest would follow, and I should see on the pedestal my marble queen. Then surely if my songs sufficed to give her life before, when she lay in the bonds of alabaster, much more would they be sufficient then to give her volition and motion, when she alone of assembled crowds of marble forms, would be standing rigid and cold."

===Chinese===
不用说，第二天傍晚我便开始了一趟穿越各厅堂的巡行（因为我不知道梦把我带到了哪一座），指望能发现我的大理石美人立在她的黑色基座上，从而证实那梦是真的。末了，走到第十座厅堂时，我似乎认出了梦中所见舞蹈着的几个身影；而令我大惑不解的是，当我走到最左边的角落，那里立着的——是我至今见过的唯一一座——空基座。它的位置，恰是梦里白衣女郎的基座所占的位置。希望在我心头剧烈地跳动。

「现在，」我暗自说，「倘若那梦还有另一部分也能应验，让我得以乘这些形体夜舞之不备，出其不意地撞进它们中间；那么其余的部分或许会接踵而至，我便会看见基座上立着我的大理石女王。到那时，既然从前她躺在雪花石膏的束缚里，我的歌声足以赋予她生命，那么如今，当满堂聚集的大理石形体中唯独她一人僵立在那儿、又冷又硬的时候，我的歌声定然更加足以赋予她意志和行动。」

===Original===
But the difficulty was, to surprise the dancers. I had found that a premeditated attempt at surprise, though executed with the utmost care and rapidity, was of no avail. And, in my dream, it was effected by a sudden thought suddenly executed. I saw, therefore, that there was no plan of operation offering any probability of success, but this: to allow my mind to be occupied with other thoughts, as I wandered around the great center-hall; and so wait till the impulse to enter one of the others should happen to arise in me just at the moment when I was close to one of the crimson curtains. For I hoped that if I entered any one of the twelve halls at the right moment, that would as it were give me the right of entrance to all the others, seeing they all had communication behind. I would not diminish the hope of the right chance, by supposing it necessary that a desire to enter should awake within me, precisely when I was close to the curtains of the tenth hall.

At first the impulses to see recurred so continually, in spite of the crowded imagery that kept passing through my mind, that they formed too nearly a continuous chain, for the hope that any one of them would succeed as a surprise. But as I persisted in banishing them, they recurred less and less often; and after two or three, at considerable intervals, had come when the spot where I happened to be was unsuitable, the hope strengthened, that soon one might arise just at the right moment; namely, when, in walking round the hall, I should be close to one of the curtains.

===Chinese===
但难就难在，要乘舞者不备。我已经发现，预谋的偷袭，哪怕执行得再小心再迅疾，也归于无用。而在我的梦里，成功靠的是一个骤然想到的念头骤然实行。于是我看明白了：没有任何行动计划有成功的可能，除非这样——我绕着那座白色中央大厅漫游时，任凭心思被别的念头占据；这样等着，直到要进某一座厅堂的冲动，恰巧在我走近某幅深红帷幔的一瞬在我心里升起。因为我盼望，只要时机恰好，我从十二座厅堂的任何一座进去，便仿佛取得了进入其余各座的资格，因为它们在后面全都彼此相通。我决不愿把侥幸的希望减少分毫，去设想那个进去的欲望必须恰恰在我走近第十厅的帷幔时才在我心里醒来。

起初，那想看的冲动不顾我心头不断涌过的纷繁意象，一再反复来临，几乎连成一条不断的链，哪一次都难望成为乘人不备的一击。但我坚持把它们驱散，它们便来得越来越少；有两三次，隔着颇长的间隔来了，却都赶在我恰好站在不合适的地方的时候；于是希望增强了：也许不久便有一次，恰在合适的时刻升起——那就是，当我绕着大厅走着，恰好走近某一幅帷幔的时候。

===Original===
At length the right moment and the impulse coincided. I darted into the ninth hall. It was full of the most exquisite moving forms. The whole space wavered and swam with the involutions of an intricate dance. It seemed to break suddenly as I entered, and all made one or two bounds towards their pedestals; but, apparently on finding that they were thoroughly overtaken, they returned to their employment (for it seemed with them earnest enough to be called such) without further heeding me. Somewhat impeded by the floating crowd, I made what haste I could towards the bottom of the hall; whence, entering the corridor, I turned towards the tenth. I soon arrived at the corner I wanted to reach, for the corridor was comparatively empty; but, although the dancers here, after a little confusion, altogether disregarded my presence, I was dismayed at beholding, even yet, a vacant pedestal. But I had a conviction that she was near me. And as I looked at the pedestal, I thought I saw upon it, vaguely revealed as if through overlapping folds of drapery, the indistinct outlines of white feet. Yet there was no sign of drapery or concealing shadow whatever. But I remembered the descending shadow in my dream. And I hoped still in the power of my songs; thinking that what could dispel alabaster, might likewise be capable of dispelling what concealed my beauty now, even if it were the demon whose darkness had overshadowed all my life.

===Chinese===
终于，那合适的时刻和那股冲动重合了。我一头扑进第九厅。厅中满是至精美妙的活动的形体。整个空间随着一支繁复舞蹈的回旋而摇曳、而流转。我进去时，舞蹈仿佛蓦地一裂，众人都向各自的基座跃去一两步；但显然发觉自己已被彻底撞破，她们便重新回到那营生里去（因为在她们，那似乎郑重得配称作营生），不再理会我。浮动的人群稍有阻滞，我竭力抢向厅堂的尽头；从那里进入长廊，转向第十厅。我很快便到了要到达的那个角落，因为长廊里比较空；可是，虽然这里的舞者在起初一阵慌乱之后，全然不再理会我的在场，我仍旧沮丧地看见，那里立的还是一个空基座。但我心里确信她就在近旁。我凝视那基座时，仿佛看见基座上，隔着层层叠叠如帷衣的褶皱依稀显影，有几只白足朦胧的轮廓。然而无论帷衣还是遮蔽的影子，都毫无迹象。但我想起了梦里那道垂落的黑影。我依旧寄望于我歌声的力量；心想：既然这力量能驱散雪花石膏，或许也能驱散此刻遮蔽我的美人的东西，即便那正是那魔影——它的黑暗曾笼罩我一生的魔影。

## XV / 第十五章

===Original===
And now, what song should I sing to unveil my Isis, if indeed she was present unseen? I hurried away to the white hall of Phantasy, heedless of the innumerable forms of beauty that crowded my way: these might cross my eyes, but the unseen filled my brain. I wandered long, up and down the silent space: no songs came. My soul was not still enough for songs. Only in the silence and darkness of the soul’s night, do those stars of the inward firmament sink to its lower surface from the singing realms beyond, and shine upon the conscious spirit. Here all effort was unavailing. If they came not, they could not be found.

===Chinese===
此刻，我该唱一支怎样的歌，才能揭开我的伊西丝（Isis）的面纱呢——倘若她当真隐身临在？我匆匆赶往幻想的白殿，无心理会一路上簇拥而来的无数美的形影：它们尽可掠过我的眼，我的头脑里却满满是那未见之物。我在那静默的空间里久久徘徊，来来回回：没有歌来临。我的灵魂还不够宁静，生不出歌。唯有在灵魂之夜的寂静与幽暗里，内在苍穹的那些星辰，才会从彼岸的歌唱之界沉降到苍穹的低处表面，照临醒觉的心灵。在这里，一切努力都归于无效。它们若不肯来临，便无从寻得。

===Original===
Next night, it was just the same. I walked through the red glimmer of the silent hall; but lonely as there I walked, as lonely trod my soul up and down the halls of the brain. At last I entered one of the statue-halls. The dance had just commenced, and I was delighted to find that I was free of their assembly. I walked on till I came to the sacred corner. There I found the pedestal just as I had left it, with the faint glimmer as of white feet still resting on the dead black. As soon as I saw it, I seemed to feel a presence which longed to become visible; and, as it were, called to me to gift it with self-manifestation, that it might shine on me. The power of song came to me. But the moment my voice, though I sang low and soft, stirred the air of the hall, the dancers started; the quick interweaving crowd shook, lost its form, divided; each figure sprang to its pedestal, and stood, a self-evolving life no more, but a rigid, lifelike, marble shape, with the whole form composed into the expression of a single state or act. Silence rolled like a spiritual thunder through the grand space. My song had ceased, scared at its own influences. But I saw in the hand of one of the statues close by me, a harp whose chords yet quivered. I remembered that as she bounded past me, her harp had brushed against my arm; so the spell of the marble had not infolded it. I sprang to her, and with a gesture of entreaty, laid my hand on the harp. The marble hand, probably from its contact with the uncharmed harp, had strength enough to relax its hold, and yield the harp to me. No other motion indicated life.

===Chinese===
第二夜，情形一模一样。我走过那静默大殿的红色微光；我在那里面行走时多么孤独，我的灵魂在脑的诸殿中上上下下踱步时，也就同样孤独。终于，我走进了一座雕像之殿。舞蹈刚刚开场，我欣喜地发觉，这一次我可以不受禁阻地置身于他们的聚会之间。我一路走去，直到那神圣的角落。基座仍如我离开时一般，那一抹仿佛白色双足的幽微光晕，依然停在死寂的黑色之上。我一望见它，便似乎感到有一个渴望现形的临在；它仿佛在呼唤我，求我赋它以自我显现之力，好让它得以照耀在我身上。歌唱之力来到了我身上。可我的声音刚一搅动大殿的空气——虽然我唱得又低又柔——舞者们便都悚然一惊；那疾速交织的人群震颤起来，失了队形，四下散开；每个形体都跃回自己的基座，伫立在那里，不再是自我演化的生命，而是一具僵硬却栩栩如生的大理石形骸，整个身姿凝定为单一情态或动作的表达。寂静像一阵精神的雷鸣，滚过这恢弘的空间。我的歌声停了，被它自己的效力吓住了。但我看见近旁一尊雕像的手里，有一架竖琴，琴弦犹自颤动。我想起她从我身旁跃过时，她的竖琴曾擦过我的手臂；所以大理石的魔咒不曾把它裹住。我向她扑去，带着恳求的手势，把手按在竖琴上。那大理石的手——大约因为接触了未受魔咒的竖琴——尚有余力松开把握，把竖琴让给了我。除此之外，再没有任何动作显示生命。

===Original===
Instinctively I struck the chords and sang. And not to break upon the record of my song, I mention here, that as I sang the first four lines, the loveliest feet became clear upon the black pedestal; and ever as I sang, it was as if a veil were being lifted up from before the form, but an invisible veil, so that the statue appeared to grow before me, not so much by evolution, as by infinitesimal degrees of added height. And, while I sang, I did not feel that I stood by a statue, as indeed it appeared to be, but that a real woman-soul was revealing itself by successive stages of imbodiment, and consequent manifestation and expression.

===Chinese===
我出于本能拨响琴弦，唱了起来。为了不打断我这支歌的记录，我在此说明：当我唱出头四行时，那双最可爱的脚已在那黑色基座上清晰显现；而我每唱下去，便仿佛有一重面纱正从那形体前面掀起——却是一重看不见的面纱，以致那雕像像是正在我眼前生长，与其说是演化而成，不如说是以无穷细微的增量节节增高。而且，当我歌唱时，我并不觉得自己站在一座雕像旁——尽管看上去确乎如此——而是觉得一个真实的女性灵魂，正借着赋形的层层阶段，以及随之而来的显现与表达，一步一步揭示它自己。

===Original===
> Feet of beauty, firmly planting
 

 Arches white on rosy heel!
 

 Whence the life-spring, throbbing, panting,
 

 Pulses upward to reveal!
 

 Fairest things know least despising;
 

 Foot and earth meet tenderly:
 

 ’Tis the woman, resting, rising
 

 Upward to sublimity,

===Chinese===
> 美的双足，牢牢踏定
 

 白色的足弓，落在玫瑰色的脚跟上！
 

 生命之泉由此涌出，悸动着、喘吁着，
 

 向上搏动，以求显现！
 

 至美之物最少懂得轻贱；
 

 足与大地温柔相接：
 

 这是女人，栖止着，又升起
 

 向上，达于崇高之境，

===Original===
> Rise the limbs, sedately sloping,
 

 Strong and gentle, full and free;
 

 Soft and slow, like certain hoping,
 

 Drawing nigh the broad firm knee.
 

 Up to speech! As up to roses
 

 Pants the life from leaf to flower,
 

 So each blending change discloses,
 

 Nearer still, expression’s power.

===Chinese===
> 肢体升起，安详地倾斜，
 

 强健而温柔，丰盈而自由；
 

 柔缓，像一种笃定的希冀，
 

 渐渐挨近那宽阔坚实的膝。
 

 向上而臻于言语！正如生命
 

 从叶到花，向玫瑰喘息而上，
 

 这一道道交融的变幻，显露出
 

 愈来愈近的表达之力。

===Original===
> Lo! fair sweeps, white surges, twining
 

 Up and outward fearlessly!
 

 Temple columns, close combining,
 

 Lift a holy mystery.
 

 Heart of mine! what strange surprises
 

 Mount aloft on such a stair!
 

 Some great vision upward rises,
 

 Curving, bending, floating fair.

===Chinese===
> 看哪！优美的弧线，白色的浪涛，
 

 无所畏惧地盘升、外展！
 

 神殿的廊柱，紧密相依，
 

 托举起一桩神圣的奥秘。
 

 我的心呀！何等奇异的惊喜
 

 正沿这样的阶梯攀上高处！
 

 有什么伟大的异象冉冉升起，
 

 曲折、俯仰，曼妙地浮动。

===Original===
> Bands and sweeps, and hill and hollow
 

 Lead my fascinated eye;
 

 Some apocalypse will follow,
 

 Some new world of deity.
 

 Zoned unseen, and outward swelling,
 

 With new thoughts and wonders rife,
 

 Queenly majesty foretelling,
 

 See the expanding house of life!

===Chinese===
> 一束束弧带，一处处丘壑起伏，
 

 牵着我看迷了的眼；
 

 随后将有启示降下，
 

 将有神性的新世界。
 

 无形的环带束着，向外隆起，
 

 满孕新的思想与奇观，
 

 预告着女王般的威仪——
 

 看哪，生命之屋正在扩展！

===Original===
> Sudden heaving, unforbidden
 

 Sighs eternal, still the same—
 

 Mounts of snow have summits hidden
 

 In the mists of uttered flame.
 

 But the spirit, dawning nearly,
 

 Finds no speech for earnest pain;
 

 Finds a soundless sighing merely—
 

 Builds its stairs, and mounts again.

===Chinese===
> 突然的起伏，那不受禁阻的
 

 永恒叹息，始终如一——
 

 雪的峰峦把峰顶藏进
 

 倾吐出的火焰的雾霭里。
 

 但那将近破晓的精灵，
 

 寻不出言语说出恳切的痛苦；
 

 只寻得一声无声的叹息——
 

 便筑起阶梯，再度攀登。

===Original===
> Heart, the queen, with secret hoping,
 

 Sendeth out her waiting pair;
 

 Hands, blind hands, half blindly groping,
 

 Half inclasping visions rare;
 

 And the great arms, heartways bending;
 

 Might of Beauty, drawing home;
 

 There returning, and re-blending,
 

 Where from roots of love they roam.

===Chinese===
> 心，那女王，怀着隐秘的希冀，
 

 派出她守候着的一双；
 

 手，盲的手，半是盲然摸索，
 

 半已把珍异的幻象轻揽；
 

 而伟大的双臂，向心弯拢；
 

 美之伟力，引它们归家；
 

 归到那里，重新交融，
 

 归到它们自爱之根漫游而出的地方。

===Original===
> Build thy slopes of radiance beamy
 

 Spirit, fair with womanhood!
 

 Tower thy precipice, white-gleamy,
 

 Climb unto the hour of good.
 

 Dumb space will be rent asunder,
 

 Now the shining column stands
 

 Ready to be crowned with wonder
 

 By the builder’s joyous hands.

===Chinese===
> 筑起你光芒粲然的坡垒吧，
 

 精灵，因女人气质而美！
 

 竖起你白光闪闪的绝壁，
 

 向那良善的时辰攀登。
 

 哑默的空间将被撕裂，
 

 看哪，光辉的巨柱已然立定，
 

 正待由建造者欢悦的双手
 

 加冕以惊奇。

===Original===
> All the lines abroad are spreading,
 

 Like a fountain’s falling race.
 

 Lo, the chin, first feature, treading,
 

 Airy foot to rest the face!
 

 Speech is nigh; oh, see the blushing
 

 Sweet approach of lip and breath!
 

 Round the mouth dim silence, hushing,
 

 Waits to die ecstatic death.

===Chinese===
> 一切线条向四方舒展，
 

 像喷泉洒落的一族。
 

 看，下颏，第一个容貌特征，踏步而来，
 

 是让脸庞栖足的轻盈之足！
 

 言语近了；哦，看那红晕里
 

 唇与气息甜美的趋近！
 

 嘴周朦胧的寂静，渐渐平息，
 

 等待着在心醉神迷中死去。

===Original===
> Span across in treble curving,
 

 Bow of promise, upper lip!
 

 Set them free, with gracious swerving;
 

 Let the wing-words float and dip.
 

 *Dumb art thou?* O Love immortal,
 

 More than words thy speech must be;
 

 Childless yet the tender portal
 

 Of the home of melody.

===Chinese===
> 弯成三重的弧拱吧，
 

 应许之弓，上唇！
 

 以优雅的偏转释放它们；
 

 让带翅的话语浮翔、俯冲。
 

 *你哑默么？* 哦，不死的爱，
 

 你的言语必胜过言词；
 

 那旋律之家的温柔门扉
 

 还未曾有过孩子。

===Original===
> Now the nostrils open fearless,
 

 Proud in calm unconsciousness,
 

 Sure it must be something peerless
 

 That the great Pan would express!
 

 Deepens, crowds some meaning tender,
 

 In the pure, dear lady-face.
 

 Lo, a blinding burst of splendor!—
 

 ’Tis the free soul’s issuing grace.

===Chinese===
> 如今鼻翼无畏地张开，
 

 在平静的无自觉中傲然，
 

 确信必是某种绝世无双之物
 

 才当得伟大的潘神（Pan）来表达！
 

 有什么温柔的意涵加深、聚拢，
 

 在那纯洁可爱的淑女面容上。
 

 看，一片炫目的光辉迸发！——
 

 那是自由的灵魂启程而出的恩泽。

===Original===
> Two calm lakes of molten glory
 

 Circling round unfathomed deeps!
 

 Lightning-flashes, transitory,
 

 Cross the gulfs where darkness sleeps.
 

 This the gate, at last, of gladness,
 

 To the outward striving *me*:
 

 In a rain of light and sadness,
 

 Out its loves and longings flee!

===Chinese===
> 两泓平静的熔金般的荣光之湖，
 

 环绕着不可测度的深渊！
 

 转瞬即逝的电光闪闪，
 

 掠过黑暗沉眠的深壑。
 

 这终于便是欢悦之门，
 

 通向那向外挣扎的*我*：
 

 在一阵光与哀伤的雨里，
 

 它的爱与渴慕夺路而出！

===Original===
> With a presence I am smitten
 

 Dumb, with a foreknown surprise;
 

 Presence greater yet than written
 

 Even in the glorious eyes.
 

 Through the gulfs, with inward gazes,
 

 I may look till I am lost;
 

 Wandering deep in spirit-mazes,
 

 In a sea without a coast.

===Chinese===
> 一个临在将我击中，
 

 使我哑然，带着预知的惊奇；
 

 那临在之伟大，甚至胜过
 

 那双荣耀的眼睛里所写下的。
 

 透过深壑，以内视的目光，
 

 我尽可凝望，直到迷失自己；
 

 深深漫游于精神的迷宫，
 

 在一片没有海岸的海里。

===Original===
> Windows open to the glorious!
 

 Time and space, oh, far beyond!
 

 Woman, ah! thou art victorious,
 

 And I perish, overfond.
 

 Springs aloft the yet Unspoken
 

 In the forehead’s endless grace,
 

 Full of silences unbroken;
 

 Infinite, unfeatured face.

===Chinese===
> 窗向着荣耀敞开！
 

 哦，远远在时间与空间之外！
 

 女人，啊！你胜利了，
 

 而我，爱得过深，就此灭亡。
 

 那尚未说出者向上涌起，
 

 在前额无尽的恩泽之中，
 

 满是未曾打破的静默；
 

 无限而无相的脸。

===Original===
> Domes above, the mount of wonder;
 

 Height and hollow wrapt in night;
 

 Hiding in its caverns under
 

 Woman-nations in their might.
 

 Passing forms, the highest Human
 

 Faints away to the Divine:
 

 Features none, of man or woman,
 

 Can unveil the holiest shine.

===Chinese===
> 上方的穹顶，惊奇的峰峦；
 

 高处与凹谷裹在夜色里；
 

 在它底下的洞窟中，藏着
 

 威仪赫赫的女子族类。
 

 流转的诸形之中，至高的人性
 

 淡去而化入神性：
 

 无论男子的还是女子的容貌，
 

 都揭不开那至圣的光辉。

===Original===
> Sideways, grooved porches only
 

 Visible to passing eye,
 

 Stand the silent, doorless, lonely
 

 Entrance-gates of melody.
 

 But all sounds fly in as boldly,
 

 Groan and song, and kiss and cry,
 

 At their galleries, lifted coldly,
 

 Darkly, ’twixt the earth and sky.

===Chinese===
> 在侧面，只有刻着凹槽的门廊，
 

 为掠过的眼睛所见：
 

 静默、无门、孤单的
 

 旋律入口之门立在那里。
 

 但一切声响都放胆飞入，
 

 呻吟与歌，亲吻与哭喊，
 

 飞向它们的回廊——那回廊冷冷架起，
 

 幽幽地悬在天地之间。

===Original===
> Beauty, thou art spent, thou knowest:
 

 So, in faint, half-glad despair,
 

 From the summit thou o’erflowest
 

 In a fall of torrent hair;
 

 Hiding what thou hast created
 

 In a half-transparent shroud:
 

 Thus, with glory soft-abated,
 

 Shines the moon through vapory cloud.

===Chinese===
> 美，你耗尽了，你自己知道：
 

 于是，怀着微弱而半喜的绝望，
 

 你从峰顶漫溢而下，
 

 化作一头急瀑般的长发；
 

 把你所创造的一切
 

 藏进一重半透明的裹巾：
 

 于是，荣光柔和地敛了，
 

 月亮便这样透过雾霭的云照耀。

## XVI / 第十六章

===Original===
> Selbst der Styx, der neunfach sie umwindet,
> Wehrt die Rückkehr Ceres Tochter nicht;
> Nach dem Apfel greift sie, und es bindet
> Ewig sie des Orkus Pflicht
> Ev’n the Styx, which ninefold her infoldeth
> Hems not Ceres’ daughter in its flow;
> But she grasps the apple—ever holdeth
> Her, sad Orcus, down below.

===Chinese===
> Selbst der Styx, der neunfach sie umwindet,
> Wehrt die Rückkehr Ceres Tochter nicht;
> Nach dem Apfel greift sie, und es bindet
> Ewig sie des Orkus Pflicht
> 纵是九重缠裹她的斯提克斯，
> 它的流水也围不住刻瑞斯的女儿；
> 但她攫住了那苹果——悲哀的俄耳库斯便永远
> 把她攫在幽冥之下。

===Original===
Ever as I sang, the veil was uplifted; ever as I sang, the signs of life grew; till, when the eyes dawned upon me, it was with that sunrise of splendor which my feeble song attempted to re-imbody. The wonder is, that I was not altogether overcome, but was able to complete my song as the unseen veil continued to rise. This ability came solely from the state of mental elevation in which I found myself. Only because uplifted in song, was I able to endure the blaze of the dawn. But I cannot tell whether she looked more of statue or more of woman; she seemed removed into that region of phantasy where all is intensely vivid, but nothing clearly defined. At last, as I sang of her descending hair, the glow of soul faded away, like a dying sunset. A lamp within had been extinguished, and the house of life shone blank in a winter morn. She was a statue once more—but visible, and that was much gained. Yet the revulsion from hope and fruition was such, that, unable to restrain myself, I sprang to her, and, in defiance of the law of the place, flung my arms around her, as if I would tear her from the grasp of a visible Death, and lifted her from the pedestal down to my heart. But no sooner had her feet ceased to be in contact with the black pedestal, than she shuddered and trembled all over; then, writhing from my arms, before I could tighten their hold, she sprang into the corridor, with the reproachful cry, “You should not have touched me!” darted behind one of the exterior pillars of the circle, and disappeared. I followed almost as fast; but ere I could reach the pillar, the sound of a closing door, the saddest of all sounds sometimes, fell on my ear; and, arriving at the spot where she had vanished, I saw, lighted by a pale yellow lamp which hung above it, a heavy, rough door, altogether unlike any others I had seen in the palace; for they were all of ebony, or ivory, or covered with silver-plates, or of some odorous wood, and very ornate; whereas this seemed of old oak, with heavy nails and iron studs. Notwithstanding the precipitation of my pursuit, I could not help reading, in silver letters beneath the lamp: “No one enters here without the leave of the Queen.” But what was the Queen to me, when I followed my white lady? I dashed the door to the wall and sprang through. Lo! I stood on a waste windy hill. Great stones like tombstones stood all about me. No door, no palace was to be seen. A white figure gleamed past me, wringing her hands, and crying, “Ah! you should have sung to me; you should have sung to me!” and disappeared behind one of the stones. I followed.
